essay ^ArxhPHvj

henry lawson brings to light the disparity between
power dynamics of different gender roles.
the villainous looking sundowner, bushman in the
horrors, and the gallows-faced swagman uses
gothic terminology to create a supernaturalistic 
terrifying image of other men, highlighting the
perspective of a women in the 1890s, who view
unknown men as the same as a gothic genre
character view a monster

lawson advocates for women having the ability to
develop masculine traits when needed to
her surroundings are not favourable to developing
her 'feminine' or her sentimental side
- fighting flood, pneumonia, bushfires
- she is used to being alone.

however, lawson believes that women romanticise 
the idea of femininity and housewife, and the
masculine role isn't cut out for them.
- she has love for her children, but no time to
show it
- she "dresses up" the children often, even though
there is no external need.
- young ladies journal, where the wife finds all the
"excitement and recreation she needs"
-  ^te62xFYy

intro: many texts of the past which are able to generate 
remarkably progressive ideas of the past, but falls short of modern
ideals. The Drovers Wife (1892) written by Henry Lawson is such a text,
by transforming a masculine archetype to fit into an icon of female strength,
the wife has to protect the farm and the family. ^1PG32iYc

2Cu+ + 2e- -> 2Cu (s) ^KbSmiSmZ

		{
			"id": "te62xFYy",
			"type": "text",
			"x": 1294.189448222203,
			"y": -224.84952546508072,
			"width": 499.7795715332031,
			"height": 675,
			"angle": 0,
			"strokeColor": "#1e1e1e",
			"backgroundColor": "transparent",
			"fillStyle": "solid",
			"strokeWidth": 0.5,
			"strokeStyle": "solid",
			"roughness": 1,
			"opacity": 100,
			"groupIds": [],
			"frameId": null,
			"roundness": null,
			"seed": 666687533,
			"version": 1407,
			"versionNonce": 1788744675,
			"isDeleted": false,
			"boundElements": null,
			"updated": 1715592523649,
			"link": null,
			"locked": false,
			"text": "henry lawson brings to light the disparity between\npower dynamics of different gender roles.\nthe villainous looking sundowner, bushman in the\nhorrors, and the gallows-faced swagman uses\ngothic terminology to create a supernaturalistic \nterrifying image of other men, highlighting the\nperspective of a women in the 1890s, who view\nunknown men as the same as a gothic genre\ncharacter view a monster\n\nlawson advocates for women having the ability to\ndevelop masculine traits when needed to\nher surroundings are not favourable to developing\nher 'feminine' or her sentimental side\n- fighting flood, pneumonia, bushfires\n- she is used to being alone.\n\nhowever, lawson believes that women romanticise \nthe idea of femininity and housewife, and the\nmasculine role isn't cut out for them.\n- she has love for her children, but no time to\nshow it\n- she \"dresses up\" the children often, even though\nthere is no external need.\n- young ladies journal, where the wife finds all the\n\"excitement and recreation she needs\"\n- ",
			"rawText": "henry lawson brings to light the disparity between\npower dynamics of different gender roles.\nthe villainous looking sundowner, bushman in the\nhorrors, and the gallows-faced swagman uses\ngothic terminology to create a supernaturalistic \nterrifying image of other men, highlighting the\nperspective of a women in the 1890s, who view\nunknown men as the same as a gothic genre\ncharacter view a monster\n\nlawson advocates for women having the ability to\ndevelop masculine traits when needed to\nher surroundings are not favourable to developing\nher 'feminine' or her sentimental side\n- fighting flood, pneumonia, bushfires\n- she is used to being alone.\n\nhowever, lawson believes that women romanticise \nthe idea of femininity and housewife, and the\nmasculine role isn't cut out for them.\n- she has love for her children, but no time to\nshow it\n- she \"dresses up\" the children often, even though\nthere is no external need.\n- young ladies journal, where the wife finds all the\n\"excitement and recreation she needs\"\n- ",
			"fontSize": 20,
			"fontFamily": 1,
			"textAlign": "left",
			"verticalAlign": "top",
			"baseline": 668,
			"containerId": null,
			"originalText": "henry lawson brings to light the disparity between\npower dynamics of different gender roles.\nthe villainous looking sundowner, bushman in the\nhorrors, and the gallows-faced swagman uses\ngothic terminology to create a supernaturalistic \nterrifying image of other men, highlighting the\nperspective of a women in the 1890s, who view\nunknown men as the same as a gothic genre\ncharacter view a monster\n\nlawson advocates for women having the ability to\ndevelop masculine traits when needed to\nher surroundings are not favourable to developing\nher 'feminine' or her sentimental side\n- fighting flood, pneumonia, bushfires\n- she is used to being alone.\n\nhowever, lawson believes that women romanticise \nthe idea of femininity and housewife, and the\nmasculine role isn't cut out for them.\n- she has love for her children, but no time to\nshow it\n- she \"dresses up\" the children often, even though\nthere is no external need.\n- young ladies journal, where the wife finds all the\n\"excitement and recreation she needs\"\n- ",
			"lineHeight": 1.25
		},

			"isDeleted": false,
			"boundElements": null,
			"updated": 1715592523649,
			"link": null,
			"locked": false,
			"text": "intro: many texts of the past which are able to generate \nremarkably progressive ideas of the past, but falls short of modern\nideals. The Drovers Wife (1892) written by Henry Lawson is such a text,\nby transforming a masculine archetype to fit into an icon of female strength,\nthe wife has to protect the farm and the family.",
